Yes. I understand the broad vision, but I **won’t start designing the product, architecture, feature list, or tech stack yet**. We should first lock the product decisions that materially affect all of those.

### Question 1 — Who actually controls the trades?

There are two fundamentally different products we could build:

**A. Assisted trading platform — my recommendation for V1**

The website:
- helps the user construct an option/F&O strategy
- shows expected P&L, margin, payoff graph, Greeks, breakevens, etc.
- monitors the live market
- detects when predefined conditions are met
- tells the user **“Your strategy is ready / adjustment recommended / exit condition reached”**
- can prepare the orders
- user confirms/authorizes the trade

Then progressively introduce automation where appropriate.

**B. Autonomous trading platform**

The user defines the strategy and conditions once, and the system can independently:
- monitor the market
- enter positions
- place multi-leg orders
- adjust positions
- exit positions
- repeat the process without asking the user each time.

### My recommendation

I would **not make full autonomy the starting point**, even though I absolutely would design the architecture so it can eventually support it.

For your target user—someone with basic option-selling knowledge—the killer experience should initially be:

> **“Tell me what you want to achieve → I'll help you construct the strategy → show me exactly what can happen → monitor it → tell me what action makes sense → let me execute it easily.”**

Then automation becomes an **opt-in progression**, rather than making the entire product feel like a complicated algo-trading terminal.

This also lets us focus the UX around **strategies rather than individual orders**.

For example:

> **NIFTY Iron Condor**  
> Entry: When NIFTY is between X–Y  
> Max profit: ₹8,400  
> Max loss: ₹11,600  
> Adjustment: If NIFTY crosses X → shift call spread  
> Exit: 50% profit OR loss reaches ₹5,000 OR 3:15 PM  
>  
> **[Start Strategy]**

rather than forcing the user to think in terms of four separate option orders.

**My recommendation: A for V1, architected from day one so B can be introduced later.**

### Question 1

Which direction do you want?

**A — Assisted first, automation later (recommended)**  
**B — Full autonomous trading from V1**  
**C — Both from V1, with the user choosing the level of automation**

### Question 3 — What should the user actually start with?

This is probably the **most important UX decision** for the entire product.

When a user enters the website and wants to create a trade, I see three possible approaches:

**A. Strategy-first — my strong recommendation**

The user chooses:

> “I want to create a strategy”

Then the system guides them:

**Market:** NIFTY / SENSEX  
→ **View:** Bullish / Bearish / Range-bound / Neutral  
→ **Risk:** Conservative / Moderate / Aggressive  
→ **Strategy:** system recommends suitable strategies  
→ **Configure:** expiry, strikes, quantity, etc.  
→ **Preview:** payoff + profit/loss + margin + risk  
→ **Execute**

For example:

> **I think NIFTY will stay in a range**  
>   
> Recommended strategies:  
> 🟢 Iron Condor  
> 🟢 Short Strangle  
> 🟡 Short Straddle  
>   
> **Why?**  
> “These strategies can benefit if NIFTY remains within the expected range.”

This is ideal for your “anyone with basic knowledge can use it” objective.

---

**B. Leg/order-first**

The user starts by selecting:

> Buy/Sell → CE/PE → Strike → Expiry → Quantity

This is familiar to experienced traders but quickly becomes complicated for beginners.

---

**C. Both**

Give users:

> **Create Strategy** | **Build Manually**

Beginners use Strategy-first; experienced traders can construct their own multi-leg positions.

### My recommendation: **C, but Strategy-first should be the default**

The important distinction is that **“strategy” becomes the primary object in the product**, not an order.

The system should understand:

**Strategy → Legs → Entry conditions → Risk → Adjustments → Exit conditions → Orders**

rather than simply:

**Orders → Orders → Orders → Orders**

That decision will make the later automation, adjustment engine, P&L tracking and broker integration much cleaner.

## Question 4 — Should the system recommend a strategy?

This is an important boundary.

Suppose a user says:

> **“I think NIFTY will stay between 24,500 and 25,000 this week.”**

Should the website actively help them decide *what strategy to use*?

There are three possibilities:

### A. Strategy calculator only

The user chooses:

> Iron Condor

The platform helps them construct, analyse, monitor and execute it.

It **doesn't recommend** whether an Iron Condor, Strangle, Straddle, etc. is appropriate.

### B. Guided strategy discovery — **my recommendation**

The user tells the platform something simple:

> **Market view:** Range-bound  
> **Risk:** Moderate  
> **Expected move:** ₹500  
> **Capital:** ₹2 lakh

The platform says something like:

> **Based on your inputs, here are strategies you could evaluate:**
>
> 1. Iron Condor — defined risk
> 2. Short Strangle — higher premium, higher risk
> 3. Iron Fly — narrower range, higher reward potential

It explains **why each appears**, shows the payoff/risk and lets the user choose.

Crucially, it doesn't pretend:

> “This is the strategy you should trade.”

Instead:

> **“Here are strategies that match the parameters you entered. Review the risks before trading.”**

### C. AI trading advisor

The user could essentially ask:

> “What should I sell on NIFTY today?”

And the system analyses live market conditions, IV, Greeks, trend, events, etc., and recommends a specific trade.

I **do not recommend making C the foundation of V1**. It adds significant complexity and, more importantly, changes the product from a strategy/execution platform into something much closer to an investment/trading recommendation product.

We can potentially introduce carefully constrained intelligence later.

### My recommendation

**B — Guided strategy discovery.**

It fits your goal beautifully:

> **User thinks in simple terms → platform translates that into strategies → user understands the trade → user decides → platform helps execute/manage it.**

---

### Question 4

Choose:

**A — No recommendations**  
**B — Guided strategy discovery (recommended)**  
**C — AI actively recommends trades**

# Question 5 — How much should the platform automate?

This is different from our first question. We already decided **assisted trading for V1**. Now we need to define *what “assisted” actually means*.

Imagine a user has created this strategy:

> **NIFTY Iron Condor**
>
> Entry condition: NIFTY between 24,700–24,900  
> Premium target: ₹8,000+  
> Max loss: ₹12,000  
> Exit: 50% profit  
> Adjustment: If NIFTY crosses 25,100  
> Time exit: 3:00 PM

When the conditions are met, what should happen?

### Option A — Alert only

The system says:

> 🔔 **Strategy condition met**
>
> Your Iron Condor can now be entered.

User opens the strategy and manually places the orders.

**Safest and simplest V1.**

---

### Option B — Prepare orders — **my recommendation**

When conditions are met:

> 🟢 **Entry conditions met**
>
> Strategy ready.
>
> 4 orders will be placed:
> - Sell NIFTY 25,000 CE
> - Buy NIFTY 25,100 CE
> - Sell NIFTY 24,600 PE
> - Buy NIFTY 24,500 PE
>
> Estimated margin: ₹X  
> Maximum loss: ₹Y  
> Maximum profit: ₹Z
>
> **[Review & Execute]**

The platform prepares everything, but **the user gives the final confirmation**.

This can make the product dramatically easier without giving V1 unrestricted control of someone's account.

---

### Option C — One-time authorization

User says:

> **“For this strategy, I'm authorizing automatic entry when my conditions are satisfied.”**

The system can then trigger the orders automatically, subject to predefined safeguards.

The user still has explicit control over the strategy and its rules.

This would be a logical **V1.5/V2** capability.

---

### Option D — Fully autonomous strategy lifecycle

The system can independently:

**Detect → Enter → Monitor → Adjust → Re-enter → Exit**

according to the strategy's rules.

This is the ultimate vision you described, but I would **not start here**.

---

### My recommendation

I'd build the platform in this progression:

**Level 1 — Alert**

> “Your condition is met.”

↓

**Level 2 — Prepare**

> “I've constructed the exact orders for you.”

↓

**Level 3 — Confirm**

> “Review → Execute.”

↓

**Level 4 — Controlled Auto**

> “You have authorized this strategy to execute automatically.”

↓

**Level 5 — Autonomous management**

> “The strategy can adjust and exit according to its predefined rules.”

This gives us a very powerful product without making the initial experience intimidating.

---

# Question 6 — Which brokers should we support?

This is a major architectural decision because broker integration affects authentication, order execution, live data, reliability, and ultimately how easy the product is to use.

You mentioned **“any major/top 5–10 brokers.”**

I would *not* try to integrate 10 brokers initially.

My recommendation would be to build a **Broker Adapter Layer**:

```text
                    Your Platform
                         │
                Broker Adapter Layer
                         │
        ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
        ↓                ↓                ↓
     Zerodha          Angel One       Upstox
        │                │                │
      Orders           Orders           Orders
      Data             Data             Data
```

Your strategy engine doesn't care which broker the user has.

It simply says:

> “Sell NIFTY 25,000 CE × 75”

The broker adapter translates that into the specific broker's API requirements.

### For V1, I see two possibilities:

**A — Zerodha only**

Build the best possible integration first.

Advantages:
- simpler
- faster development
- excellent ecosystem
- very large Indian active-trader user base

Then add Angel One, Upstox, etc.

**B — Zerodha + Angel One from V1 — my recommendation for your situation**

Because your existing business ecosystem already involves these two brokers, I think supporting both from the beginning gives you a much stronger real-world test.

Then architect:

**Phase 2:** Upstox  
**Phase 3:** 5–10 major brokers based on actual customer demand.

I don't want us to make the mistake of saying:

> “We support 10 brokers”

while having fragile integrations everywhere.

I'd rather have:

> **2 excellent broker integrations**

than 10 unreliable ones.

---

### One more important point

I would **not make users enter broker API keys manually** if we can avoid it.

The desired UX should eventually be:

> **Connect your broker**
>
> [ Zerodha ]  
> [ Angel One ]  
> [ Upstox ]  
> [ Others ]

→ Authenticate with broker  
→ Return to our platform  
→ Account connected.

The user should never need to understand APIs, access tokens, WebSockets, etc.

---

# Question 8 — What instruments should V1 actually support?

You originally mentioned:

- Call Buy
- Call Sell
- Put Buy
- Put Sell
- Futures Buy
- Futures Sell
- Multiple legs / combinations
- NIFTY
- SENSEX

There's an important distinction here.

I recommend that the **underlying universe remain extremely small in V1**:

> **NIFTY + SENSEX only**

But within those, the strategy builder should support **individual legs**, so users can construct virtually any combination:

### Options

- Buy CE
- Sell CE
- Buy PE
- Sell PE

### Futures

- Buy Future
- Sell Future

### Multi-leg

For example:

**Bull Put Spread**
- Sell PE
- Buy lower-strike PE

**Iron Condor**
- Sell CE
- Buy higher CE
- Sell PE
- Buy lower PE

**Covered/hedged futures strategy**
- Future + options

And eventually users can combine options + futures into more sophisticated structures.

### My recommendation

Don't create a separate menu containing 30 named strategies.

Instead, have:

> **Strategy Templates**

and

> **Manual Builder**

The templates make things easy for beginners, while the builder gives experienced users freedom.

For example:

**Popular strategies**

🟢 Bull Put Spread  
🟢 Bear Call Spread  
🟢 Iron Condor  
🟢 Iron Fly  
🟢 Calendar Spread  
🟢 Straddle  
🟢 Strangle  
🟢 Covered strategies  
🟢 Custom Strategy

But underneath, everything is built from the same **six basic instruments/actions**.

That will make the system much more scalable.

# Question 9 — How should we handle live market data?

This is a **critical architecture and cost decision**, and I don't want to assume the answer.

You want users to see things like:

> **NIFTY 25,180.40 ▲ 0.42%**  
> Spot  
>   
> Expected move: ±₹420  
>   
> ATM: 25,200  
>   
> Expiry: Thursday  
>   
> **Option Chain**
>
> | Strike | CE LTP | CE OI | PE LTP | PE OI |
> |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
> | 25,000 | ₹xxx | xxx | ₹xxx | xxx |
> | 25,100 | ₹xxx | xxx | ₹xxx | xxx |
> | 25,200 | ₹xxx | xxx | ₹xxx | xxx |

The question is **where this live data should come from**.

There are two broad architectures.

### A — Broker-provided market data

When the user connects Zerodha/Angel One/etc., we use that broker's market-data APIs/WebSockets.

For example:

> User connected Zerodha → Zerodha feed  
> User connected Dhan → Dhan feed

**Advantages**
- potentially lower infrastructure/data cost
- data already associated with the user's broker connection
- broker-specific execution and market data can stay aligned

**Problems**
- different brokers provide different data capabilities
- user may not have connected a broker yet
- your website's market display could behave differently depending on broker
- supporting six feeds becomes more complicated

---

### B — Dedicated market-data provider — **my recommendation**

Have a **central market-data service** for your platform:

```text
             Market Data Provider
                     ↓
              Market Data Engine
                     ↓
        ┌────────────┼────────────┐
        ↓            ↓            ↓
      Website     Strategy      Alerts
                   Engine
                     ↓
              Broker Adapters
```

Then the user's broker connection is primarily used for:

> **Account → Orders → Positions → Holdings → Execution**

rather than making your entire application's market-data layer dependent on whichever broker the user happens to connect.

This is much cleaner for the product.

It also means:

> User visits your website without connecting any broker

and can still explore:

- NIFTY
- SENSEX
- option chain
- strategy builder
- payoff
- hypothetical P&L
- strategy simulation

Then they connect a broker only when they want to trade.

---

### One important caveat

For an Indian trading product, we need to be very careful about **market-data licensing, redistribution rights, exchange requirements and broker/API terms**.

So I don't want to simply say:

> “Let's scrape NSE.”

**We won't.**

When we get to the architecture stage, I'll research the currently available licensed market-data/API options and the relevant requirements before recommending the actual provider.

---

## My recommendation

**Centralized licensed market-data layer + broker APIs for account/order/execution.**

Broker feeds can potentially be used as a secondary/fallback source where appropriate, but **the product shouldn't fundamentally depend on them for its user experience**.

This also makes the system much easier to scale from 2 brokers to 6+.

---

**B — Centralized market-data provider + broker APIs for trading — recommended**

**C — Hybrid: centralized feed where possible + broker feeds as backup**

# Question 10 — How should users define a strategy?

Now we're getting into the **core UX**, and I think this is where your product can become genuinely different from existing trading platforms.

I see two ways.

### A — Traditional trading form

The user sees something like:

> Underlying: NIFTY  
> Expiry: 25 Sep  
> Strategy: Iron Condor  
> Sell CE: 25,500  
> Buy CE: 25,600  
> Sell PE: 24,700  
> Buy PE: 24,600  
> Quantity: 75  
> Entry price: Market  
> Stop loss: ₹X  
> Target: ₹Y

This is efficient for experienced traders but **not beginner-friendly**.

---

### B — Conversational / guided strategy builder — **my recommendation**

Instead of asking 15 technical questions at once:

### Step 1

> **What are you expecting NIFTY to do?**
>
> 🟢 Go Up  
> 🔴 Go Down  
> 🟡 Stay in a Range  
> ⚪ Not Sure

User selects:

**Stay in a Range**

### Step 2

> **How much risk are you comfortable taking?**
>
> 🟢 Lower risk  
> 🟡 Moderate  
> 🔴 Higher risk

### Step 3

> **How much capital do you want to use?**

₹2,00,000

### Step 4

The system shows:

> ### Strategies you could consider
>
> **Iron Condor**
> Defined maximum loss  
> ⭐ Beginner-friendly
>
> **Short Strangle**
> Higher premium potential  
> ⚠️ Higher risk
>
> **Iron Fly**
> Works best around a narrower range  
> ⚠️ More sensitive to movement
>
> **[Compare]**

User chooses **Iron Condor**.

Then the platform automatically proposes strikes:

> **Suggested setup**
>
> NIFTY 25,500 CE — Sell  
> NIFTY 25,600 CE — Buy  
> NIFTY 24,700 PE — Sell  
> NIFTY 24,600 PE — Buy
>
> **Estimated credit:** ₹8,250  
> **Max profit:** ₹8,250  
> **Max loss:** ₹1,750  
> **Margin:** ₹52,000
>
> **[Adjust Strategy]**

And *then* the user can change strikes, expiry, quantity, etc.

---

### Why I strongly prefer B

The user should **not need to understand how to construct an Iron Condor before using your platform.**

Your software should translate:

> **“I think NIFTY will stay in a range and I don't want unlimited risk.”**

into:

> **“Here are three structures you can evaluate.”**

That is the product's biggest potential UX advantage.

And importantly, the advanced trader can click:

> **Advanced → Manual Builder**

and get the traditional leg-by-leg interface.

---

## One important product principle

I would **not hide the numbers**.

Even beginners should always be able to see:

- Maximum profit
- Maximum loss
- Breakeven
- Required margin
- Capital at risk
- Estimated charges
- Probability metrics where appropriate
- Payoff graph
- What happens if the underlying moves ±1%, ±2%, etc.

But we present them in **plain language first**, with technical details expandable.

For example:

> ### What can I lose?
> **Up to ₹12,450**
>
> ### What can I make?
> **Up to ₹8,200**
>
> ### Where do I start losing?
> **Below 24,680 or above 25,520**
>
> `View Greeks & advanced analytics →`

That's much more human than starting with:

> Delta = -0.08  
> Gamma = -0.002  
> Theta = +₹1,840  
> Vega = -₹3,200

The Greeks are still there—but **they don't dominate the experience.**

# Question 11 — How should strategy adjustments work?

This is particularly important because you specifically mentioned that users should be able to **identify and make adjustments**.

I see three levels.

### A — Manual adjustments only

The platform monitors the strategy and says:

>

>
> Current P&L: -₹3,240
>

The user decides what to do.

---

### B — Guided adjustments — **my recommendation**

The system detects the condition and explains possible actions.

For example:

> ⚠️ **Adjustment opportunity**
>
> NIFTY has moved 1.1% above your original range.
>
> Your Iron Condor is now under pressure.
>
> ### Possible actions
>
> **1. Move Call Spread**
> Move 25,500/25,600 → 25,700/25,800
>
> Estimated impact:
> +₹1,850 additional credit  
> New breakeven: 24,680–25,790
>
> **2. Reduce Risk**
> Close the call side
>
> Estimated realized loss: ₹2,400
>
> **3. Exit Strategy**
> Close all positions
>
> **[Compare Adjustments]**

Then the user selects one and the platform prepares the required orders.

This is much more powerful than simply saying:

> “NIFTY crossed 25,500.”

---

### C — Automatic adjustment

The user defines:

> If NIFTY > 25,500 → move call spread

The system automatically executes the adjustment.

This is something we can support later when controlled automation is introduced.

---

## My recommendation

Build **B into V1**.

The strategy engine should understand that a strategy isn't finished once the entry order is placed.

It has a lifecycle:

```text
PLAN
  ↓
WAIT FOR ENTRY
  ↓
ENTER
  ↓
MONITOR
  ↓
┌───────────────┐
│                │
Normal          Condition Triggered
│                │
↓                ↓
MONITOR      SUGGEST ADJUSTMENT
                 ↓
              USER DECIDES
                 ↓
             PREPARE ORDERS
                 ↓
               EXECUTE
                 ↓
              MONITOR
                 ↓
                EXIT
```

That becomes one of your major differentiators.

# Question 15 — How should the platform handle the actual strategy calculations?

This sounds technical, but it directly affects what users can trust.

For every strategy, I think the platform should be able to show:

- Entry credit/debit
- Maximum profit
- Maximum loss
- Breakeven points
- Required margin
- Approximate charges
- Current P&L
- P&L at different NIFTY/SENSEX levels
- Payoff graph
- Greeks
- Position-level and strategy-level P&L
- What happens after an adjustment
- What happens if the strategy is exited now

The question is whether we make the calculations:

### A — Simple/basic

Calculate primarily based on entry/exit prices and payoff.

Easy to build, but insufficient for a serious options platform.

### B — Full options strategy/risk engine — **my recommendation**

Build a dedicated calculation engine that understands:

> **Underlying → expiry → option contract → legs → quantity → premium → Greeks → payoff → margin → charges → current market value → strategy P&L**

The key concept would be:

### **One calculation engine**

The same engine powers:

**Strategy Builder**

→ “What will happen if I create this?”

**Order Preview**

→ “What exactly am I about to trade?”

**Live Strategy**

→ “What is happening now?”

**Adjustment Simulator**

→ “What happens if I move this leg?”

**Exit**

→ “What happens if I close now?”

This avoids the nightmare where the strategy builder says one thing and the live position screen calculates something differently.

---

### C — Use a third-party options analytics engine

Potentially faster initially, but introduces:

- vendor dependency
- ongoing cost
- data consistency issues
- less control
- potentially difficult customization

I would only use external services where they provide something we genuinely shouldn't build ourselves.

---

## My recommendation

**B — Build our own strategy/risk calculation engine**, while using properly sourced market data and broker APIs.

But I don't mean reinventing every financial model ourselves.

For example, the engine can use established libraries/formulas for:

- Black-Scholes
- Greeks
- payoff
- implied volatility
- probability calculations

while **our domain engine** owns the strategy lifecycle and calculations.

---

### One very important design principle

We should **never show false precision**.

For example, instead of:

> “Probability of profit = 73.48291%”

we should present something appropriate to the calculation method and data quality, such as:

> **Estimated probability of profit: ~73%**

and clearly explain the assumptions.

The user needs to understand:

> **These are estimates, not guarantees.**

---

## Question 15

Which approach do you want?

**A — Basic calculator**  
**B — Dedicated strategy/risk engine — strongly recommended**  
**C — Third-party analytics engine**

I strongly recommend **B**.

- implied volatility

>

>

> Maximum loss: ₹1,750  
> Capital required: ₹52,000
>

> - Fits your selected risk level
> - Short strikes are outside the estimated move
> - Defined maximum loss
>
> [Use This] [Adjust]

The user can then move strikes and immediately see the impact.

>

> Wider protection, lower premium
>
> 🟡 **Balanced**
> Moderate protection/premium
>
> 🔴 **Aggressive**
> Higher premium, less protection
>
> **Or customize manually →**

Then the system proposes the actual strikes.

This keeps the interface extremely simple.

---

**C**

But internally it's powered by the same strike-selection engine.

So the user isn't forced to understand:

> Delta 0.15  
> 1.5 standard deviations  
> IV percentile 72  
> etc.

They can simply say:

> **Conservative**

and see what that means financially.

Advanced users can switch to:

> **Custom → choose strikes manually**

---

### One important principle

The platform should **never silently select a trade and place it**.

It should say:

> **“Suggested setup”**

not:

> **“Recommended trade”**

and clearly show the assumptions behind the suggestion.

# Question 17 — How should users define when to enter a strategy?

This is one of the most important decisions because it connects the **planning engine** to the **alert/order-preparation engine**.

### A — Enter immediately

User builds the strategy and clicks:

> **Prepare Orders**

Simple, but not very powerful.

>

>

The platform monitors those conditions.

>
> Iron Condor is ready.
>

This fits our **Alert + Prepare Orders** decision perfectly.

### C — Advanced condition builder

Experienced users could create combinations such as:

> IF NIFTY crosses 25,100  
> AND IV percentile > 60  
> AND premium > ₹180  
> AND time > 10:30  
> THEN prepare strategy

This is powerful, but exposing a rule engine like this to beginners would make the product intimidating.

### D — Both B + C — **my recommendation for the eventual product**

The user sees simple conditions:

> **When should I enter?**
>
> Market reaches my range  
> Premium reaches my target  
> Volatility is high  
> At a specific time  
> Immediately

Then:

> **Advanced conditions →**

Experienced users can build compound rules.

---

### The important UX idea

The user should **not have to create conditions at all**.

For example:

> **Enter now**

can simply be the default.

Or:

> **Wait for better premium**

could automatically let them specify:

> “Enter when total premium reaches ₹8,000.”

So the system progressively introduces complexity only when needed.

- entry alerts

- exit alerts
- future automation

# Question 18 — How should the user define when to exit?

For an options-selling product, I consider this even more important than entry.

A user might want to exit because:

- target profit reached
- maximum acceptable loss reached
- underlying crosses a level
- premium falls to a target
- expiry/time is approaching
- strategy reaches a particular P&L
- adjustment condition occurs
- user wants to exit manually

>

>

> IF P&L ≥ ₹6,000

> → Prepare exit orders

And later, when autonomous trading is introduced, the exact same rules can trigger actual execution subject to the user's authorization.

That is much more aligned with your product's purpose and is particularly important for an option-selling platform.

# Question 19 — Should users be able to test a strategy before using real money?

I think this is **very important for your target audience**, especially beginners.

Imagine someone creates:

> **NIFTY Iron Condor**  
> Conservative  
> ₹2 lakh capital

Before they prepare real orders, we could offer:

For example:

> **Last 30 trading days**
>
> 📈 Best outcome: +₹12,400  
> 📉 Worst outcome: −₹8,700  
> 📊 Winning days: 21/30  
> 🔻 Maximum drawdown: ₹9,200  
>
> **[View Simulation]**

And visually:

> NIFTY movement  
> +  
> Strategy payoff  
> +  
> What would have happened at each adjustment/exit condition

### There are three levels we could support:

**A — No historical testing**

Only show theoretical payoff and current scenario analysis.

**B — Historical simulation — my recommendation**

Let users test a strategy against historical NIFTY/SENSEX data.

For example:

> “What if I had used this strategy every Thursday for the last 6 months?”

This would be extremely useful for learning and strategy validation.

**C — Full professional backtesting engine**

Allow:

- historical tick/intraday data
- entry rules
- adjustment rules
- exit rules
- slippage
- brokerage
- taxes/charges
- execution assumptions
- multiple positions
- portfolio-level testing
- optimization

This is powerful, but it can become a **whole separate product** and significantly complicate V1.

### My recommendation: **B**

I would absolutely include **strategy simulation**, but **not build a giant quant backtesting platform initially**.

The first version could simply answer:

> **“If these rules had been applied historically, how would this strategy have behaved?”**

And importantly, label it clearly as a **simulation**, not proof that the strategy will work in the future.

---

## Why I think this matters for your product

It creates a very nice learning loop:

> **Build → Understand → Simulate → Modify → Compare → Plan → Trade**

A beginner doesn't need to blindly trust a strategy suggestion.

They can ask:

> **“Show me what would have happened.”**

And an experienced trader gets a useful validation tool.

# Question 20 — What should “live strategy monitoring” actually monitor?

Once a user has entered a strategy, I want the system to treat the **whole strategy as one object**, rather than making them watch individual legs.

For example:

> ### NIFTY Iron Condor
> **₹ +4,280**
>
> Entry: ₹8,250 credit  
> Current value: ₹3,970  
> Profit captured: 52%
>
> 🟢 **Healthy**
>
> NIFTY: 24,980  
> Your range: 24,700–25,300
>
> **Risk**
> Max loss: ₹12,000  
> Current risk: ₹4,200
>
> **Plan**
> 🎯 Target: ₹6,000  
> 🛑 Max loss: ₹8,000  
> ⏰ Exit: 3:00 PM
>
> **[View Strategy]**

Then the system continuously evaluates the strategy against its plan.

I see three levels of monitoring:

### A — Basic P&L monitoring

Just:

> Current P&L  
> Target  
> Stop loss

Simple but limited.

### B — Strategy-health monitoring — **my recommendation**

The system evaluates:

- P&L
- underlying movement
- distance from breakevens
- distance from short strikes
- time to expiry
- IV changes
- Greeks
- margin utilization
- entry conditions
- adjustment conditions
- exit conditions

Then translates that into simple status:

🟢 **Healthy**

🟡 **Watch**

🟠 **Adjustment opportunity**

🔴 **Exit condition reached**

This is much more useful than making users stare at Greeks.

### C — Advanced predictive monitoring

The platform predicts:

> “Based on current movement, your strategy has a high likelihood of hitting the upper breakeven within X hours.”

I would **not make this part of V1**.

It can be explored later, but prediction can easily create a false sense of certainty.

---

## My recommendation: **B**

The key concept is:

> **Don't show the user 20 indicators. Tell them whether their strategy is behaving according to the plan they created.**

For example:

> 🟡 **Watch**
>
> NIFTY has moved closer to your upper short strike.
>
> No adjustment condition has been triggered yet.
>
> **Distance:** 1.2%  
> **Your adjustment trigger:** 0.8%
>
> You don't need to act yet.

That's the sort of calm, contextual UX I want for this product.

---

**B — Strategy-health monitoring — recommended**

# Question 23 — Whose money and whose broker account can be traded?

This is a very important boundary for both product design and the eventual regulatory/compliance analysis.

My strong recommendation is:

### **The platform should only allow a user to trade their own connected broker account.**

In other words:

> User signs up → connects **their own** Zerodha/Dhan/etc. account → creates strategy → platform prepares orders → user reviews → orders go to **that user's account**.

We should **not** design V1 around:

- managing other people's trading accounts
- pooled money
- copying one user's trades into other users' accounts
- taking discretionary control of customer portfolios
- users giving us broker credentials
- employees manually trading customer accounts

Instead, the product remains a **technology platform that helps the customer plan, analyse, monitor and prepare trades in their own account**.

### Why I'm recommending this

It keeps the product's core promise very clean:

> **“Your account. Your strategy. Your decision. Our technology.”**

It also fits perfectly with the assisted-trading model we've already chosen.

Later, if you want to introduce more advanced automation, we can separately examine what permissions, broker requirements, SEBI/exchange rules, consent mechanisms, audit trails and other controls are required.

I don't want us to assume those requirements now—we'll explicitly research them before the architecture/business model is finalized.

# Question 26 — How should the platform handle order execution?

This is particularly important because we're dealing with **multi-leg option strategies**.

Suppose the user has an Iron Condor and clicks:

> **Execute Strategy**

There are multiple ways we could handle this.

### A — Sequential execution

Place each leg one after another.

Simple, but introduces **legging risk**.

For example:

1. Sell CE
2. Buy CE
3. Sell PE
4. Buy PE

The market can move between those orders.

---

### B — Basket/multi-leg execution

Prepare the entire strategy as a basket and send the legs together using Zerodha's available order mechanisms.

**My recommendation for V1.**

The user sees something like:

> **Review Order**
>
> Sell 75 NIFTY 25,000 CE  
> Buy 75 NIFTY 25,300 CE  
> Sell 75 NIFTY 24,700 PE  
> Buy 75 NIFTY 24,400 PE  
>
> Estimated margin: ₹1.42L  
> Estimated credit: ₹8,250  
> Estimated charges: ₹___  
>
> **Review & Place**

Then the user explicitly confirms.

---

### C — Smart execution

The platform decides the optimal order sequence based on liquidity, spreads, risk and market conditions.

Powerful, but **I don't recommend this for V1**. It adds considerable complexity and can create execution-risk questions.

---

### D — User chooses the execution method

Advanced users can choose:
- sequential
- basket
- custom leg order

Useful eventually, but unnecessarily complicated for the initial product.

---

## My recommendation: **B**

The core principle would be:

> **One strategy → one order plan → one confirmation → controlled multi-leg execution.**

The system should also handle situations such as:

- One leg fills and another doesn't
- Partial fills
- Order rejection
- Price moving before execution
- Insufficient margin
- Broker/API timeout

Instead of simply saying **“Order failed,”** the platform should tell the user:

> ⚠️ **Strategy partially executed**  
> 3 of 4 legs completed.  
> Your current position is temporarily exposed.  
> **Review & complete strategy →**

This becomes an important part of the product's safety architecture.

Which execution model do you want?

=== #56 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T11:42 ===
Yes — **your thinking is correct**, and I would actually refine my previous recommendation because of this.

For a defined-risk option-selling strategy, **execution sequence matters**, not just whether we call it a “basket.”

### Example

Suppose the strategy is:

- Buy 24,500 PE
- Sell 24,800 PE
- Buy 25,300 CE
- Sell 25,000 CE

If we sell the two short options first:

> Sell PE → margin gets blocked  
> Sell CE → more margin required  
> Buy protective legs → potentially insufficient available margin

That can leave the user with an undesirable intermediate state.

But if we establish the protective legs first:

> **Buy PE → Buy CE → Sell PE → Sell CE**

the bought options can reduce the margin requirement for the subsequent short legs.

So I agree with your underlying principle:

> **For defined-risk multi-leg option strategies, establish the risk-protection/buy legs before the premium-selling legs whenever the strategy structure allows it.**

### But there's an important nuance

I **wouldn't hard-code “all buys first, then all sells” as the universal rule.**

The execution engine should understand the **relationship between legs**.

For example:

**Defined-risk spread**

`BUY protective leg → SELL short leg`

**Iron Condor**

`BUY OTM PE → SELL PE → BUY OTM CE → SELL CE`

Potentially, depending on Zerodha's margin behavior and execution mechanics, we may want to establish **both protective legs first**, then place the short legs.

But for a futures-based or naked strategy, the optimal sequence could be different.

### What I recommend we build

Instead of:

> Strategy → Orders → send all simultaneously

we build:

> **Strategy → Execution Plan → Leg Dependencies → Execution Sequence → Orders**

The platform can automatically determine:

1. Which legs provide margin/risk protection
2. Which legs depend on those positions
3. Which orders should be placed first
4. What happens if one order doesn't fill
5. Whether the remaining orders should continue, pause, or require user confirmation

And the user sees this before execution:

> **Execution plan**
>
> **Step 1 — Establish protection**
> - Buy NIFTY 24,500 PE
> - Buy NIFTY 25,300 CE
>
> **Step 2 — Establish short positions**
> - Sell NIFTY 24,800 PE
> - Sell NIFTY 25,000 CE
>
> ✓ Expected margin after protection: ₹XX,XXX  
> ⚠️ Orders will be executed in this sequence to reduce margin/legging risk.
>
> **Review & Execute**

That is much closer to the product we're trying to build.

### One more important point

We should **verify the exact Zerodha margin/order behavior with current Kite APIs and Zerodha's basket/margin mechanisms** before implementing this. We shouldn't assume that placing individual buy orders first will always produce the exact margin benefit we expect in every situation.

So I'm updating my recommendation for Q26:

**Not simply “B: basket execution.”**

Instead:

> **B+ — Intelligent, dependency-aware multi-leg execution, with protective legs established first where appropriate, followed by dependent short legs, with explicit handling of partial fills and failures.**

The user still gets **one “Execute Strategy” action**, but our execution engine handles the sequencing underneath.

I think this is a **significant improvement** to the product design.

=== #58 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T11:44 ===
Yes. I strongly agree, and I would make this a **hard execution gate**, not merely an informational warning.

Your requirement adds an important layer to the execution architecture:

### Pre-execution margin gate

Before **any order is submitted to Zerodha**, the platform should:

1. Fetch the user's **current available margin/funds from Zerodha**.
2. Calculate the margin requirement for the **planned execution sequence**.
3. Account for the fact that margin can change after each filled leg.
4. Verify that sufficient margin exists for the **next order**.
5. Only then submit that order.
6. After execution, re-check the broker's actual margin before proceeding to the next dependent order.

So the flow becomes:

**Strategy → Execution Plan → Broker Margin Check → Execute Leg → Broker Confirmation → Re-check Margin → Next Leg**

### Example

Suppose:

**Available Zerodha margin: ₹1,00,000**

The strategy requires:

- Buy protection → ₹20,000
- Buy second protection → ₹20,000
- Sell first option → ₹45,000
- Sell second option → ₹40,000

The system shouldn't simply say:

> "Total strategy margin = ₹85,000. Execute."

Instead it should understand the **actual execution sequence and broker margin state**.

After each fill:

> **Zerodha says available margin = ₹XX,XXX**

Then decide whether the next order can safely be submitted.

### And there's an important safety rule

If Zerodha says the required margin isn't available:

> 🛑 **Execution paused**
>
> Zerodha currently shows ₹XX,XXX available margin, but the next order requires approximately ₹YY,YYY.
>
> **No further order has been submitted.**
>
> [Review Strategy] [Add Funds] [Modify Quantity]

That last sentence is particularly important:

**The platform should never assume that its own margin calculation is more authoritative than the broker's actual margin state.**

Our engine can **estimate/plan** margin.  
The broker is the **execution authority**.

---

### I'm therefore adding this to our locked architecture

**Execution Safety Layer**

- Broker's real-time available margin check
- Pre-order margin validation
- Dependency-aware leg sequencing
- Re-validation after fills
- Partial-fill handling
- Order rejection handling
- Broker-confirmed order status
- No blind order submission
- Strategy-level execution state

This is exactly the kind of detail that will make the product feel trustworthy rather than just being another options calculator.

**I consider your margin-verification requirement locked into the V1 specification.**

And because this materially changes my earlier Q26 recommendation, I would now define Q26 as:

> **Intelligent, dependency-aware multi-leg execution with broker-verified margin gates before and during execution.**

We can move to **Question 27** next.

## Question 27 — What should happen if one leg fails or only partially executes?

This is one of the most important safety decisions for a multi-leg options platform.

Example:

The strategy has 4 legs:

1. Buy PE ✅
2. Buy CE ✅
3. Sell PE ❌ rejected
4. Sell CE ⏳ pending

Now the user's actual broker position **doesn't match the intended strategy**.

### My recommendation: **Pause + protect + ask the user**

The platform should immediately recognize:

> 🔴 **Strategy partially executed**

Then:

1. **Stop submitting additional dependent orders** unless they're explicitly safe.
2. Re-fetch actual Zerodha positions/orders.
3. Determine the user's current exposure.
4. Show the user exactly what happened.
5. Present the safest available actions:
   - Complete the strategy
   - Cancel remaining orders
   - Close the already-filled legs
   - Retry failed leg
6. Re-check margin before any new order.

For example:

> **Strategy partially executed**
>
> 3 of 4 legs are active.  
> Your current position differs from the planned strategy.
>
> **Current exposure:** ₹X  
> **Additional margin required:** ₹Y
>
> **What would you like to do?**
>
> 🟢 Complete Strategy  
> 🔵 Close Partial Position  
> 🟡 Retry Failed Leg  
> ⚪ Review Manually

### Why I recommend this

I **don't recommend automatically improvising** when execution goes wrong.

For example, if a short option fails, the system shouldn't blindly decide:

> "I'll sell another strike instead."

That changes the user's intended strategy.

The system should be **smart enough to protect the user from execution problems, but not so autonomous that it starts making trading decisions on their behalf.**

This fits perfectly with our existing V1 principle:

> **The system prepares and protects; the user decides.**

**A. Automatically retry failed orders**  
**B. Pause, explain the situation, and let the user choose the next action — Recommended**  
**C. Automatically close all successfully executed legs**  
**D. Something else**

=== #62 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T13:00 ===
I agree with your thinking, with **one important refinement**: the system should treat margin as a **pre-execution gate**, but still re-check broker state after fills because broker margin can change for reasons other than our own orders.

Your proposed behavior is actually better aligned with the product philosophy.

### I would design the failure flow this way

Suppose a 4-leg strategy is being executed:

1. Buy PE ✅
2. Buy CE ✅
3. Sell PE ✅
4. Sell CE ❌

The system should **not automatically unwind the three successful legs**.

Instead:

> 🔴 **Strategy partially executed**
>
> 3 of 4 legs successfully executed.
>
> Your strategy is incomplete.
>
> **Recommended next action: Complete Strategy**
>
> [Complete Strategy]  
> [Retry Failed Leg]  
> [Review Manually]  
> [Close Partial Strategy]

### Why "Complete Strategy" should be first

I agree with you because the user's original intent was:

> **Execute this strategy.**

If three legs are already successfully executed, reversing them may actually create another set of unnecessary orders, costs and execution risk.

So the system should preserve the user's intended strategy whenever possible.

### But there's one subtle distinction

I would **not say "the fourth order cannot have a margin problem."**

Your sequencing greatly reduces that risk:

**Buy protective legs → verify → sell dependent legs**

That's excellent.

But between two orders, things can change:

- A previous order may be partially filled rather than fully filled.
- Broker margin calculation may change.
- Another position/order in the user's account may affect available margin.
- An order may be rejected for something unrelated to margin.
- Contract/price conditions can change.
- Broker API state may temporarily differ.

Therefore:

> **Pre-check prevents predictable margin failures. Broker re-check protects us against unexpected state changes.**

It doesn't mean we're expecting a margin failure.

---

# My recommended Q27 design

### Execution state machine

**1. Pre-flight check**

Before starting:

- Strategy valid?
- Market open?
- Contracts valid?
- Quantity valid?
- Sufficient Zerodha margin?
- Required funds available?
- All required legs available?
- Broker connection healthy?

If any critical check fails:

**Don't start execution.**

---

**2. Execute protective legs**

Buy legs first where they provide protection/margin benefit.

After each order:

**Verify actual Zerodha status.**

---

**3. Execute dependent short legs**

Only proceed when the required protective leg is successfully established.

---

**4. If a leg fails**

Do **not** automatically reverse successful legs.

Instead:

### 🔴 Strategy Partially Executed

**Primary action:**

> 🟢 **Complete Strategy**

Then:

> 🔄 Retry Failed Leg  
> 🔍 Review Manually  
> ❌ Close Partial Strategy

The UI should clearly show **why the leg failed** if Zerodha provides a usable rejection reason.

---

**5. If "Complete Strategy" is selected**

Before retrying/completing:

- Re-fetch Zerodha positions
- Re-fetch order status
- Recalculate remaining strategy
- Re-check margin
- Verify the missing leg
- Submit only the required remaining order(s)

So we're not blindly repeating the original order.

---

### This gives us a very good principle

> **Never unnecessarily undo a successfully executed strategy leg. First try to complete the user's intended strategy.**

And:

> **Never assume the strategy is complete merely because orders were submitted. Confirm actual broker execution.**

I strongly recommend this approach.

### Q27 is therefore locked as:

**B+, but with your priority ordering:**

**Complete Strategy → Retry Failed Leg → Review Manually → Close Partial Strategy**

with **broker-state verification before continuing**.

This is a much more mature execution model than simply "place four orders."

=== #63 USER 2026-09-14T13:13 ===
Yes, go with the recommendation.

=== #64 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T13:13 ===
Perfect. **Q27 is locked with the recommended execution-recovery model.**

We now have a strong execution architecture:

> **Pre-flight validation → Protective legs → Dependent legs → Broker verification → Complete strategy if interrupted → User-controlled recovery**

# Question 28 — What order types should V1 support?

This is the next important execution decision.

For option strategies, Zerodha supports multiple order varieties, but exposing everything immediately could make the product unnecessarily complicated.

### My recommendation: **Simple by default, with three core order types**

**1. Market**
- Execute immediately at prevailing market price.
- Useful when execution certainty matters more than exact price.

**2. Limit**
- User specifies the maximum/minimum acceptable price.
- Important for controlling slippage.

**3. Stop-loss / trigger-based orders**
- Useful for future conditional execution and risk management.
- But the UX can hide the technical complexity behind simple conditions such as:
  > "Exit if NIFTY crosses 25,500."

### I would NOT expose every Zerodha-specific order option directly in the main strategy builder.

Instead, the product should translate the user's intent:

> **"Enter when premium reaches ₹120 or better."**

into the appropriate broker order configuration.

Advanced users could eventually get:

> **Advanced Order Settings**

where they can control order type, limit price, trigger, validity, etc.

---

### One more important recommendation

For multi-leg strategies, I think we should introduce an **execution price tolerance**.

For example:

> Expected strategy credit: ₹12,500  
> Current executable credit: ₹12,350  
> Maximum acceptable slippage: ₹300  
>
> 🟢 **Within tolerance — Execute**

This is much more understandable than forcing users to think about four separate limit prices.

The system can calculate the leg-level prices while showing the user the **strategy-level outcome**.

---

=== #67 USER 2026-09-14T13:18 ===
How would you know that which option is Zerodha approved option contract?

=== #68 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T13:18 ===
That's a very good question—and it exposes an important distinction in our design.

**We should not try to determine “Zerodha-approved option contracts” ourselves from a static list.** There are actually several layers of eligibility.

### 1. First: does the contract exist?

We can obtain Zerodha's **instrument master** and build our internal contract catalogue from it. Kite Connect provides the instruments for exchanges such as NFO/BFO, and the instrument data contains the information needed to identify contracts. Zerodha's own developer guidance says the instrument dump is the source for this and it should not be polled continuously; it is updated daily. citeturn0search12turn0search15

So our system knows things like:

- NIFTY/SENSEX
- CE/PE
- strike
- expiry
- exchange
- trading symbol
- instrument token
- lot size
- etc.

### 2. But “exists” ≠ “currently tradable”

This is the important part.

Zerodha can dynamically restrict **fresh orders** based on things such as:

- Open Interest
- liquidity
- expiry
- strike
- order type
- product type
- other RMS/exchange restrictions

For example, Zerodha currently says fresh F&O orders can be blocked when OI is below the applicable threshold, and that these checks happen in real time. citeturn0search1

There are also specific restrictions for NIFTY and SENSEX option strikes. Zerodha currently describes OI-based strike restrictions and says its Kite interface displays permitted strikes when restrictions apply. citeturn0search5

So we **cannot create a permanent database saying:**

> “25,000 CE = approved”  
> “25,500 CE = not approved”

because that status can change.

---

# So how should OUR system know?

I recommend a **three-level validation system**.

### Level 1 — Our contract catalogue

Before showing a contract to the user:

**Zerodha instrument data → our database**

This answers:

> “Is this a valid Zerodha-listed contract?”

---

### Level 2 — Real-time market/risk eligibility

When the user is actually planning/executing:

We check current information such as:

- OI
- bid/ask
- liquidity
- LTP
- expiry
- strike
- available market data
- current broker restrictions that we can determine programmatically

This answers:

> **“Does this currently look eligible for the intended transaction?”**

---

### Level 3 — Zerodha is the final authority

And this is the most important layer.

**Immediately before submitting the order, Zerodha's API/order response is authoritative.**

Our system should never say:

> “Zerodha will definitely accept this order.”

Instead:

> **“Our pre-flight checks indicate this order is eligible. We're now submitting it to Zerodha.”**

If Zerodha rejects it, we capture the actual rejection reason and present it to the user.

That's especially important because Zerodha itself can dynamically apply restrictions. For example, it says OI levels are checked in real time and fresh orders can become allowed again if OI rises above the relevant threshold. citeturn0search1

---

## And this changes something in our earlier design

I don't want our strategy engine to say:

> ❌ “This is a Zerodha-approved contract.”

Instead, internally we should have something like:

**Contract status**

- `VALID_CONTRACT`
- `CURRENTLY_ELIGIBLE`
- `RESTRICTED`
- `INSUFFICIENT_LIQUIDITY`
- `UNKNOWN_REQUIRES_BROKER_CHECK`
- `ORDER_REJECTED`

That is much safer.

### Example

User creates:

> NIFTY  
> 30 Sep expiry  
> 26,000 CE Sell

Our system might show:

> **✓ Contract available**

Then:

> **✓ Current market data available**  
> **✓ Liquidity acceptable**  
> **✓ Current OI conditions acceptable**  
> **✓ Strategy margin available**

Then:

> **Ready to execute**

But at the actual order submission:

> Zerodha → **REJECTED: current broker restriction**

We don't fight the broker's decision. We stop and tell the user exactly what happened.

---

### One more useful discovery

Zerodha's own Kite option-chain interface already handles some of this complexity: it can display the available expiries/strikes, OI, market depth, etc., and Zerodha also supports baskets where execution order can be specified and initial/final margin benefits displayed. citeturn0search6

So **we should use Zerodha's APIs/data as the source of truth wherever possible**, rather than trying to reverse-engineer Zerodha's RMS rules.

And this is precisely why I think our eventual execution engine should have a component called something like:

> **Zerodha Execution Eligibility & Pre-flight Engine**

rather than a generic “order type selector.”

This is an important architectural decision, and I recommend we **lock it in**.

**Then we can return to Question 29** about whether the platform should support individual manual orders or remain strategy-first.

**NIFTY → Expiry → Strike → CE/PE → Buy/Sell**

our system should first ask Zerodha, in effect:

> ✅ Available for this strategy

The contract can be added.

If Zerodha says **no**:

> ❌ This contract cannot currently be used for this order.

And **don't allow the user to add that leg to the strategy**.

This is much cleaner than us maintaining dozens of rules ourselves.

### However, one technical caveat

We shouldn't assume Zerodha provides a single API like:

> `is_contract_allowed = true/false`

for every possible restriction.

Their restrictions include things like OI-based blocks and order-type-specific restrictions, and some restrictions are surfaced through Kite's UI/nudge mechanisms rather than necessarily through a simple pre-validation endpoint. citeturn0search1turn0search3

So our implementation should be:

**Zerodha data/API → determine what we can validate before strategy creation → Zerodha order submission remains the final authority.**

We don't need to build a parallel Zerodha RMS engine.

### And this fits our product philosophy perfectly

The user doesn't need to know:

> "Why is this strike outside Zerodha's OI threshold?"

They should simply see:

> **25,000 CE — Available ✅**

or

> **27,000 CE — Currently unavailable on Zerodha ⚠️**  
> *Zerodha isn't accepting fresh orders for this contract right now.*

And if the situation changes, we can refresh availability.

---

### One thing I would *not* do

I wouldn't permanently remove a contract from our database because Zerodha rejects it **today**.

The contract itself still exists. Its **current tradability status** can change. Zerodha explicitly says some OI restrictions are checked in real time and can become available again when conditions change. citeturn0search0

So:

**Contract catalogue ≠ current availability.**

That's the only distinction we need to maintain.

### Locked decision

**Our platform will not attempt to duplicate Zerodha's complete contract/RMS eligibility logic.**

Instead:

> **Zerodha determines whether the contract/order is currently eligible. Our strategy builder respects that result and prevents unavailable legs from being added.**

Then, immediately before execution, we still perform the broker-side validation and let Zerodha's actual order response remain authoritative.

I think this is **simpler, safer, and much more maintainable**.

- place a standalone order
- bypass strategy validation
- bypass margin checks
- bypass broker restrictions
- execute a strategy without an exit plan
- override a critical broker rejection

They simply get **more information and more control within the strategy framework**.

### One additional idea

We could allow users to choose:

> **Default experience:** Guided / Standard / Advanced

and also have:

> **“Show advanced details”**

on individual screens.

That means an Advanced user doesn't have to live in a visually overwhelming terminal all the time.

### Question 32

Should users be able to **change their experience level whenever they want**?

**A. Yes — user-controlled, with safety rules always enforced — Recommended**

**B. Let users upgrade, but require some onboarding/education first**

**C. Lock the level based on an assessment**

**D. Keep one level permanently per user**

=== #81 USER 2026-09-14T13:27 ===
A

=== #82 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T13:27 ===
Perfect. **Q32 = A is locked.**

Users can switch between **Guided / Standard / Advanced** whenever they want, while the core strategy and safety controls remain mandatory for everyone.

---

# Question 33 — How should we handle risk acknowledgement?

This is important because we're building a platform that can actually execute options trades.

I recommend **not** using a generic:

> ☑️ “I understand the risks.”

checkbox every time. Users quickly click through those.

Instead, risk acknowledgement should be **contextual**.

### Example 1 — Creating a defined-risk strategy

> **Maximum possible loss: ₹12,400**
>
> If all protective assumptions hold, your maximum strategy loss is approximately ₹12,400.
>
> **Continue**

No unnecessary warning.

### Example 2 — User increases risk

User changes:

> Max loss ₹8,000 → ₹25,000

Then:

> ⚠️ **Your strategy risk has increased**
>
> Maximum possible loss is now approximately ₹25,000.
>
> [Review Risk] [Continue]

### Example 3 — User creates something particularly risky

The platform could require explicit confirmation:

> 🔴 **High-risk strategy**
>
> This strategy can expose you to losses beyond the premium received / has materially higher risk.
>
> **I understand this risk and want to continue.**

### Example 4 — Before actual execution

Regardless of experience level, the final execution screen should clearly show:

**You are about to execute:**

- Strategy
- Capital/margin required
- Maximum loss
- Maximum profit
- Current P&L if adjustment
- Number of legs
- Execution sequence
- Broker: Zerodha

Then:

> **Review complete → Execute Strategy**

This creates a **deliberate moment before real money moves**.

---

### My recommendation

Use **risk-aware UX rather than repetitive disclaimers**.

The system should distinguish between:

🟢 Normal risk → explain  
🟡 Elevated risk → warn  
🔴 Exceptional risk → explicit acknowledgement

And **Advanced users don't get to bypass critical risk acknowledgements**.

---

## Question 33

Which approach do you want?

**A. Contextual, risk-based acknowledgement — Recommended**

**B. Mandatory risk acknowledgement before every strategy execution**

**C. One global risk acknowledgement during onboarding, then minimal warnings**

**D. Another approach**

=== #84 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T13:32 ===
Yes, I understand what you're describing, and I **don't think you need to provide a screenshot yet**. Your description is sufficient to capture the requirement.

What you're describing is more than a normal payoff graph. It's a **Strategy P&L Scenario Matrix** alongside the visual payoff graph.

### I understand it like this

For an example Iron Condor:

| Leg | Action | Instrument | Strike |
|---|---|---|---:|
| 1 | Buy | NIFTY PE | 22,800 |
| 2 | Sell | NIFTY PE | 23,000 |
| 3 | Sell | NIFTY CE | 23,200 |
| 4 | Buy | NIFTY CE | 23,400 |

Then the system calculates the strategy's combined P&L.

If current NIFTY is **23,000**, the scenario table could show:

| NIFTY at Expiry | Strategy P&L |
|---:|---:|
| 21,000 | -₹X |
| 21,100 | -₹X |
| ... | ... |
| 22,800 | -₹X |
| **23,000** | **+₹X** |
| 23,100 | +₹X |
| ... | ... |
| 23,200 | +₹X |
| ... | ... |
| 25,000 | -₹X |

And the table should clearly identify:

- **Current NIFTY level**
- **Current strategy P&L**
- **Lower breakeven**
- **Upper breakeven**
- **Maximum profit zone**
- **Maximum loss zone**

The graph and table are therefore two views of the **same underlying strategy calculation**, not two separate calculations.

### I especially like your idea of showing the market-level range

Rather than only showing:

> Max Profit = ₹X  
> Max Loss = ₹Y  
> Breakeven = 22,750 / 23,250

the user can actually answer:

> **"If NIFTY expires at 22,900, how much do I make?"**

without having to understand the payoff graph.

That's extremely valuable for the **Guided** user.

And an Advanced user can still get the detailed Greeks and other analytics.

---

### One refinement I recommend

Don't make the table permanently fixed at ±1,000 or ±2,000 points.

Instead, **the system should intelligently choose the scenario range based on the strategy**.

For example:

> Current NIFTY = 23,000  
> Lower scenario = 21,000  
> Upper scenario = 25,000  
> Interval = 100 points

But if the strikes are much closer together, the table doesn't need to waste space showing thousands of irrelevant points.

We can also make it interactive:

> **Scenario range:** ±1,000 | ±2,000 | Custom

And on mobile, the table can become horizontally scrollable while the important columns remain visible.

---

### And yes, this should be available to EVERY experience level

This is one of those things I **would not hide from beginners**.

The presentation changes:

**Guided:**

> NIFTY at expiry | You make/lose

**Standard:**

> NIFTY | P&L | % return | Breakeven

**Advanced:**

> NIFTY | P&L | % return | Greeks | IV assumptions | etc.

But the fundamental **strategy → payoff → scenario table** remains universal.

This actually reinforces your earlier philosophy:

> **The platform doesn't just tell you what strategy you're using. It lets you see exactly what can happen to your money at different market levels.**

That is a very strong UX principle.

---

## One important terminology correction

You mentioned:

> "cutoff level at which the loss will be zero or profit will be zero on both sides"

I believe what you're describing is the **breakeven level**.

For the Iron Condor there will normally be:

- **Lower breakeven**
- **Upper breakeven**

Between them, the strategy may be profitable at expiry; outside them, it becomes loss-making, subject to the exact legs/premiums.

We should make those visually prominent.

---

### So I'm updating Q33

Rather than making Q33 primarily about risk acknowledgement, we've uncovered a **core universal Strategy Analysis requirement**:

> **Every strategy must have a standardized Strategy Outcome view containing the strategy legs, payoff graph, P&L scenario matrix, current P&L, breakevens, maximum profit, maximum loss, and relevant market levels.**

Risk acknowledgement can be incorporated into that experience rather than being a separate annoying checkbox everywhere.

I recommend we **lock this requirement**.

And I have one question before we move on:

### Question 33A — Scenario table calculation

Should the table show **P&L at expiry only**, or should we also show an **estimated current P&L at each NIFTY/SENSEX level before expiry**?

My recommendation for V1:

**A. Show both, but clearly distinguish them:**
- **P&L at Expiry** → exact payoff calculation based on expiry
- **Estimated Current P&L** → model-based estimate using remaining time/IV/Greeks

This is important because **P&L before expiry is not simply the expiry payoff curve**.

Which do you want: **A, B (expiry only), or C (something else)?**

> NIFTY moves from 23,150 → 23,250

> ↓

> ↓

That last part is particularly important. **We should have one strategy calculation engine**, not separate calculations for the leg table, scenario table and payoff graph.

```text
┌──────────────────── LEG INFORMATION ────────────────────┐

└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────── LIVE POSITION ──────────────────────┐
 Entry | LTP | Entry Value | Current Value | P&L | P&L %
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────── GREEKS / IV ─────────────────────────┐
 IV | Delta | Theta | Vega
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────── STRATEGY OUTCOME ────────────────────┐
22,000 | 22,100 | 22,200 | ... | 22,900 |
23,000 ★ CURRENT | 23,100 | ... | 24,000
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌────────────── STRATEGY BREAK-EVENS ──────────────────────┐
 Lower BE | Upper BE
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Status
```

The **Lower BE** and **Upper BE** should be calculated precisely from the strategy's combined payoff rather than being arbitrary table levels.

That becomes one of the product's strongest UX features.

The **market-level columns belong to the same table**, but they are **strategy-level calculations**. Therefore, I would not repeat the same P&L number on every individual leg row. The **TOTAL STRATEGY** row is where those scenario values live.

So when you horizontally scroll:

**Leg information → Live prices → Greeks → Market scenarios → Breakevens → Status**

all remain part of **one continuous table**.

> **23,000 ★ CURRENT**

### One thing I want to correct from the earlier example

The **maximum profit is ₹6,825**, not ₹3,900. The earlier illustrative payoff numbers weren't mathematically consistent with the premiums shown.

For these four legs:

- Credit from short PE = ₹86 × 75 = ₹6,450
- Credit from short CE = ₹91.50 × 75 = ₹6,862.50
- Cost of long PE = ₹42.50 × 75 = ₹3,187.50
- Cost of long CE = ₹44 × 75 = ₹3,300

**Net credit = ₹6,825**

Therefore:

- **Maximum profit = ₹6,825**
- **Maximum loss = ₹8,175**
- Lower breakeven = **22,908.75**? Let's calculate carefully: short PE 23,000 - net credit per unit ₹91 = **22,909** approximately.
- Upper breakeven = 23,400 + ₹91 = **23,491** approximately.

So the table should ultimately calculate these dynamically rather than relying on manually entered values.

### And this reveals an important product principle

The platform should **never have separate hand-coded calculations** for:

- Leg P&L
- Strategy P&L
- Maximum profit
- Maximum loss
- Breakevens
- Scenario table
- Payoff graph

They should all come from the **same strategy calculation engine**.

That way, if the user changes the 23,000 PE from ₹86 to ₹90, **everything changes consistently**—the live P&L, scenario values, breakevens, maximum profit/loss and graph.

=== #96 ASSISTANT 2026-09-14T13:42 ===
Yes — I understand your requirement now, and you are right to call this out.

You want the **market-level columns calculated separately for every leg**, not only in the `TOTAL STRATEGY` row.

For example, for **Leg 1 — BUY NIFTY PE, strike 22,800, entry ₹42.50, quantity 75**, at a hypothetical NIFTY level of **22,000**:

- Intrinsic value at 22,000 = `22,800 − 22,000 = ₹800`
- Entry premium = `₹42.50`
- Per-unit payoff/P&L at expiry = `₹800 − ₹42.50 = ₹757.50`
- Quantity = `75`
- **Leg P&L = ₹757.50 × 75 = ₹56,812.50**

So **22,000 in Leg 1 should absolutely NOT be blank**.

And the same calculation needs to happen for **every leg × every market-level column**.

### The calculation I understand

For a **BUY Call**:

`P&L = [MAX(Market Level − Strike, 0) − Entry Price] × Quantity`

For a **SELL Call**:

`P&L = [Entry Price − MAX(Market Level − Strike, 0)] × Quantity`

For a **BUY Put**:

`P&L = [MAX(Strike − Market Level, 0) − Entry Price] × Quantity`

For a **SELL Put**:

`P&L = [Entry Price − MAX(Strike − Market Level, 0)] × Quantity`

Therefore, **every cell in the market-level section should contain a calculated number for that particular leg**.

Then the **TOTAL STRATEGY** row is simply:

`Leg 1 P&L + Leg 2 P&L + Leg 3 P&L + Leg 4 P&L`

One important point: because these are **expiry scenario calculations**, the current LTP (`₹38.20`, etc.) is **not used** in calculating the expiry payoff. The entry premium is used. The LTP belongs to the **current/live P&L** columns.

So yes, I now understand exactly what you want. **No blanks in the market-level section.**

I can recreate the entire table with every one of the four leg rows populated across every market level.

### This is now the calculation structure I believe you intended

For example, **Leg 1 at 22,000**:

`(22,800 − 22,000 − 42.50) × 75`

= `₹757.50 × 75`

= **₹56,812.50**

For **Leg 2 at 22,000**:

= `(-₹914) × 75`

= **-₹68,550**

**₹56,812.50 − ₹68,550 + ₹6,862.50 − ₹3,300 = -₹8,175**

So there are **no blank market-level cells anymore**.

One thing I deliberately left as `—` for **Lower BE / Upper BE on individual legs** is because **breakeven is a property of the combined strategy**, not of each individual leg. The strategy row therefore carries the meaningful breakeven values.

### Now let's continue.

**Question 33A — Scenario calculations**

For the market-level columns such as:

`22,000 | 22,100 | 22,200 | ... | 23,000 CURRENT | ...`

we've established that every cell should show the calculated P&L for each leg.

There are two possible meanings:

**A. Expiry scenario P&L**

> “If NIFTY is at 22,000 **at expiry**, what will this leg/strategy's P&L be?”

This is mathematically exact based on intrinsic value and the entry premium.

**B. Current estimated P&L**

> “If NIFTY moves to 22,000 **right now, before expiry**, approximately what would my position be worth?”

This requires assumptions about remaining time, IV, etc., so it is an estimate.

### My recommendation: **A as the primary table**

For your product, I strongly recommend that the market-level columns initially represent:

> **P&L at Expiry**

because the numbers are transparent, deterministic and easy for a beginner to understand.

We can later provide a separate **“Estimated P&L Now”** view using the pricing model.

**Which do you want?**

**A — Expiry P&L only**  
**B — Current estimated P&L only**  
**C — Both, with Expiry P&L as the default**

### Question 33A — What exactly should the market-level P&L columns represent?

We already agreed that every market-level cell should be populated for **every leg**, and the strategy total should be the sum of those leg P&Ls.

Now we need to decide **what the hypothetical market level means**.

For example, if NIFTY is currently **23,000** and the table has a **22,000** column:

**Option A — P&L at Expiry**  
> “If NIFTY expires at 22,000, what will my P&L be?”

This is deterministic and mathematically exact from the strikes, entry prices and quantities.

**Option B — Estimated P&L Now**  
> “If NIFTY moves to 22,000 right now, before expiry, what would my position approximately be worth?”

This requires option-pricing assumptions involving remaining time, IV, etc.

**Option C — Both**  
Keep the same market-level columns, but allow a toggle:

> **At Expiry | Estimated Now**

### My recommendation: **C — Both, with “At Expiry” as the default.**

Why? Your product is supposed to let a user understand **“What can happen to my money?”** The expiry view is the cleanest and most trustworthy baseline, while the estimated-now view becomes useful for someone actively managing a live strategy.

So the same scenario section could eventually have:

**Scenario P&L: [At Expiry ▼]**

`22,000 | 22,100 | ... | 23,000 ★ Current | ...`

and switching to **Estimated Now** recalculates the same levels using the live pricing model.

**Which do you choose: A, B, or C?**

### Question 43 — Same contract, opposite actions

Suppose the user builds a strategy containing:

- **Buy NIFTY 23,000 PE — 1 lot**
- **Sell NIFTY 23,000 PE — 1 lot**

These are the **same contract**, but they have opposite actions.

What should the platform do?

**A. Combine them into one row**  
Net the position automatically, potentially resulting in zero quantity.

**B. Keep them as two separate strategy legs — Recommended** ⭐  
Show the Buy and Sell as separate legs because they represent separate user intentions and preserve the exact strategy construction. The strategy engine can still recognize that they offset each other when calculating net exposure/risk.

**C. Automatically net/cancel them and warn the user**  
Detect that they offset and prevent or discourage the combination.

### My recommendation: **B**

I strongly recommend keeping them as **separate legs**. It is safer and more transparent for a strategy platform. Automatic netting could silently change what the user intended, especially when strategies are later modified or adjusted.

**Which do you choose: A, B, or C?**

### Question 44 — What should happen if a selected contract becomes unavailable?

Imagine the user selects a NIFTY option in the Option Chain and adds it to the Strategy Builder. A few minutes later, before execution, that contract becomes **unavailable/restricted** through Zerodha—for example, the contract can no longer be placed due to a broker restriction.

What should the platform do?

**A. Automatically replace it with the nearest valid strike**  
Fastest, but it changes the user's strategy without explicit consent.

**B. Keep the original leg and show a blocking warning — Recommended** ⭐  
> “This contract is currently unavailable for execution through Zerodha.”

Then provide options such as:
- Choose another valid contract
- Modify the strategy
- Remove the leg
- Return to Option Chain

**C. Automatically remove the leg**  
The strategy becomes invalid until the user adds another contract.

**D. Automatically suggest replacement contracts, but require the user to choose one**  
For example:
> “23,000 PE is unavailable. Available alternatives: 22,900 PE / 23,100 PE.”

### My recommendation: **D**

I think **D is better than simply blocking** because the platform's job is to make strategy construction easy. However, it must **never silently substitute a contract**.

The original selection should remain visible, clearly marked as unavailable, while the platform can intelligently show valid alternatives.

### Question 46 — How should the Option Chain handle live data refresh?

Since this is a trading-oriented application, we need to decide how frequently prices and market metrics update.

**A. Real-time streaming — Recommended** ⭐  
Use a live market-data stream/WebSocket where available. LTP, bid/ask, OI-related fields and other supported live values update continuously.

**B. Fixed polling interval**  
For example, refresh every 1–2 seconds.

**C. Hybrid — Recommended architecture**  
Use streaming for critical live fields such as **LTP/bid/ask**, while less time-sensitive fields such as OI, IV or other derived values can update at a lower frequency.

**D. Manual refresh**

### My recommendation: **C — Hybrid**

This gives us the best balance between:
- responsiveness
- infrastructure/data cost
- performance
- reliability
- avoiding unnecessary updates

The user should still *feel* that the Option Chain is live.

- intentionally increasing quantity, and

**More → Market / Learn**

For mobile, the exact navigation can be optimized separately.

### Now let's make the next decision at the architecture level.

## Question 57 — What should be the primary organizing concept of the product?

I see three possible approaches:

**A. Strategy-centric — Recommended ⭐**

Everything revolves around strategies:

**Strategy → Orders → Positions → Monitoring → Adjustment → Exit**

Positions and orders are supporting views of strategies.

This is consistent with our fundamental principle:

> **Central object = Strategy, not Order.**

---

**B. Trading-terminal-centric**

Primary navigation revolves around:

**Option Chain → Positions → Orders → Market**

Strategies are a feature layered on top.

This would feel more like Zerodha/Kite.

---

**C. Hybrid**

Strategies are the primary concept, but **Positions and Orders are equally prominent top-level areas** because users need to reconcile what is actually happening at the broker.

This may be the most practical approach for an experienced trader.

### My recommendation: **C — Hybrid**

I would keep **Strategy as the conceptual center**, while giving **Positions and Orders first-class navigation**.

That gives us:

> **Strategy = Intent**  
> **Orders = Execution**  
> **Positions = Reality**

The user should be able to move seamlessly between all three.

**Which do you choose: A, B, or C?**
